# Fact-check report

Generated from `docs/fact-check.json` by `npm run factcheck:report`. Every claim has one status:

- **verified**: a source was opened and the claim matches
- **corrected**: the claim was wrong or imprecise and was fixed (old and new value shown)
- **unverifiable**: no reliable source found; the item carries `todo: true` and shows an "Unverified" marker (2050 is speculative by design)
- **removed**: false, anachronistic or untraceable; deleted from the site

#### `in.1900.dd.economy-business.interactive-data` · interactive.data · verified

> RupeeDashboard: An agricultural labourer's average monthly wage in 1896-1900 was Rs 6.5 to 7.0 across India; a day taken as 1/30 of Rs 6.75; Seers of rice at 0.0855 rupees per seer; Seers of wheat at 0.0826 rupees per seer; Seers of jowar at 0.0606 rupees per seer; Seers of gram at 0.0741 rupees per seer

- **Note:** Wage read from the rotated appendix table in the scan (all-India row, 1896-1900: 6.5 to 7.0). The daily figure divides the midpoint by 30. Seer prices are five-year averages.
- **Source:** [The Imperial Gazetteer of India, vol. III (1907), ch. IX, Appendix table I: average monthly wage of an able-bodied agricultural labourer (p. 472), and the table of quantities sold per rupee (p. 456), via the Internet Archive](https://archive.org/details/the-imperial-gazetteer-of-india-volume-3) (primary, 2026-10-14)

#### `in.1900.dd.freedom-history.famine-1899-1900` · summary · verified

> The famine of 1899-1900: The 1899 monsoon failed over the west and centre of India. At the end of July 1900 no fewer than 6,500,000 people were on daily relief, and the Famine Commission of 1901 thought about a million deaths in British districts could be attributed to it.

- **Note:** Gazetteer vol. III ch. X 'Famine'; Wikipedia article gives the same area and mortality estimates.
- **Source:** [The Imperial Gazetteer of India, The Indian Empire, vol. III: Economic (1907), Oxford, Clarendon Press, via the Internet Archive](https://archive.org/details/the-imperial-gazetteer-of-india-volume-3) (primary, 2026-10-13)

#### `in.1900.dd.technology-science.plague-vaccine` · summary · verified

> Haffkine's plague vaccine: Waldemar Haffkine tested his plague vaccine on himself in Bombay in January 1897. By the turn of the century about four million people in India had received it.

- **Note:** Wikipedia's Haffkine article: self-test January 1897; 'by the turn of the 20th century ... four million'.
- **Source:** [Wikipedia: Waldemar Haffkine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Waldemar_Haffkine) (reference, 2026-10-13)

#### `in.1900.dd.technology-science.radio-waves` · summary · verified

> Bose's millimetre radio waves: In Calcutta in 1895 Jagadish Chandra Bose rang a bell and fired gunpowder from across a room with millimetre-wavelength waves. In 1900 he presented his research at the International Congress of Physics in Paris.

- **Note:** Wikipedia: Town Hall demonstration November 1895; Paris congress 1900. Britannica: radio-wave research 1894 to 1900, 23 m transmission shown in 1895.
- **Source:** [Wikipedia: Jagadish Chandra Bose](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jagadish_Chandra_Bose) (reference, 2026-10-13)
- **Source:** [Encyclopaedia Britannica: Jagadish Chandra Bose](https://www.britannica.com/biography/Jagadish-Chandra-Bose) (secondary, 2026-10-13)

#### `in.1900.dd.technology-science.railways` · summary · verified

> Railways: about 25,400 miles open: The Gazetteer's table of railway mileage open for traffic gives a total of about 25,400 miles for 1901, mostly on the broad (5 ft 6 in) and metre gauges.

- **Note:** Table II in the Gazetteer prints a total of 25,363 miles for 1901, but its gauge columns add up to 25,463; the OCR text I read has one wrong digit, so the item says 'about 25,400'. The table has no row for 1900 itself.

#### `in.1960.dd.economy-business.population-1961` · summary · corrected

> Population: The 1961 census counted 439,234,771 people (the figure in the 1985 statistical abstract; an earlier print in the 1974 abstract gives 439,072,582).

- **Note:** Corrected: the Wikipedia infobox value 438,936,918 matches neither official print. Statistical Abstract 1985 prints 439,234,771; the 1974 abstract prints 439,072,582 for the same census, so the count was revised.

- **Source:** [Statistical Abstract India 1974 (Central Statistical Organisation), table 161: Wholesale prices of certain staple articles of trade, pp. 393-395, via the Internet Archive](https://archive.org/details/dli.ministry.23183) (primary, 2026-10-14)

> RupeeDashboard: Output per person per day (GDP per capita ÷ 365; not a wage); Kilograms of rice (wholesale) at 0.6743 rupees per kg; Kilograms of wheat (wholesale) at 0.4751 rupees per kg; Kilograms of sugar (wholesale) at 0.5772 rupees per kg; Grams of gold at 11.491 rupees per gram

### world · 1900

#### `world.1900.headlines.1900-04-30` · headline · corrected

> 1900-04-30 Front-page news across U.S. newspapers: President signs act making Hawaii a U.S. territory [paraphrase]

- **Old:** Hawaii becomes an official U.S. territory
- **New:** President signs act making Hawaii a U.S. territory
- **Note:** The Organic Act was signed on 30 April 1900; the territorial government was inaugurated on 14 June. Reworded so the headline describes the signing.
- **Source:** [Wikipedia: Hawaiian Organic Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hawaiian_Organic_Act) (reference, 2026-10-12)
- **Source:** [Wikipedia: 1900 in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1900_in_the_United_States) (reference, 2026-10-12)

#### `world.1900.morning.light-kerosene-lamp` · detail · corrected

> 5:00 AM Light the kerosene lamp: Farmhouses had no electricity: as late as 1920 only about 2 in 100 farm homes had electric service, so light came from kerosene lamps and candles.

- **Old:** Most rural homes had no electricity, so light came from kerosene lamps and candles.
- **New:** Farmhouses had no electricity: as late as 1920 only about 2 in 100 farm homes had electric service, so light came from kerosene lamps and candles.
- **Note:** Series S 109–110: only 8.0% of all dwellings had electric service in 1907 and 1.6% of farm dwellings in 1920, so a 1900 farmhouse almost certainly had none. The source starts in 1907; 1900 can only have been lower.
- **Source:** [U.S. Census Bureau, Historical Statistics of the United States, Colonial Times to 1970, series S 108–119: dwellings with electric service](https://www2.census.gov/library/publications/1975/compendia/hist_stats_colonial-1970/hist_stats_colonial-1970p2-chS.pdf) (primary, 2026-10-12)

#### `world.1900.places.newspaper` · description · corrected

> Front pages were dense columns of small type under stacked headlines. The biggest American stories of 1900 included a hurricane that killed thousands in Galveston, Texas, and a presidential election.

- **Old:** Front pages were walls of small type in narrow columns, with stacked headlines and few or no photographs. The biggest American stories of 1900 included a deadly hurricane in Texas and a presidential election.
- **New:** Front pages were dense columns of small type under stacked headlines. The biggest American stories of 1900 included a hurricane that killed thousands in Galveston, Texas, and a presidential election.
- **Note:** Event dates checked: Galveston landfall 8 September 1900 (TSHA), McKinley re-elected 6 November, Hawaiian Organic Act signed 30 April, Gold Standard Act 14 March. The claim that front pages had 'few or no photographs' was dropped: halftone photographs were already in regular use in popular journals by the early 1890s (Wikipedia: Halftone), and no source was found for newspaper photo frequency in 1900.
- **Source:** [Wikipedia: 1900 in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1900_in_the_United_States) (reference, 2026-10-12)
- **Source:** [Texas State Historical Association, Handbook of Texas: Galveston Hurricane of 1900](https://www.tshaonline.org/handbook/entries/galveston-hurricane-of-1900) (reference, 2026-10-12)
- **Source:** [Wikipedia: Halftone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Halftone) (reference, 2026-10-12)

- **New:** {"price":"13.6¢","item":"Milk, delivered (half gallon, city average)"}

> Streetcar fare (typical city fare): 5¢

- **Old:** {"price":"about $0.05","item":"Streetcar fare (in town)"}
- **New:** {"price":"5¢","item":"Streetcar fare (typical city fare)"}
- **Note:** The nickel was the flat streetcar fare in Chicago and many other cities around 1900. Only local sources were found, so the label says 'typical city fare'.

#### `world.1900.technology.kodak-brownie-camera` · description · corrected

> Brownie box camera: Launched in February 1900 for $1, it put photography into the hands of ordinary families and children.

- **Old:** {"name":"Kodak Brownie camera"}
- **New:** {"name":"Brownie box camera"}
- **Note:** Introduced February 1900 at $1; six-exposure film cartridges cost 15¢.
- **Source:** [Science Museum Group collection: No. 1 Brownie box camera](https://collection.sciencemuseumgroup.org.uk/objects/co8406729/no-1-brownie-box-camera) (reference, 2026-10-12)
- **Source:** [Lawrence W. Reed, "The Brownie Camera at 100", Foundation for Economic Education, 1 February 2000](https://fee.org/?p=37072) (secondary, 2026-10-12)

#### `world.1900.technology.mail-order-catalogue` · description · corrected

> Mail-order catalogue: Thick catalogues from two Chicago mail-order houses offered farm families city goods, from cook stoves to ready-made clothing. Free rural mail delivery, begun in 1896, was still spreading.

- **Old:** Thick catalogues brought city goods, from stoves to shoes, to rural mailboxes.
- **New:** Thick catalogues from two Chicago mail-order houses offered farm families city goods, from cook stoves to ready-made clothing. Free rural mail delivery, begun in 1896, was still spreading.
- **Note:** By 1900 Montgomery Ward and Sears were the two biggest merchandisers in the world (The Henry Ford). 'Rural mailboxes' overstated 1900: Rural Free Delivery began in 1896 and took years to reach most farms.
- **Source:** [The Henry Ford: Rural America Shops by Mail](https://www.thehenryford.org/collections/explore/articles/rural-america-shops-by-mail) (reference, 2026-10-12)
- **Source:** [Wikipedia: Rural Free Delivery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rural_Free_Delivery) (reference, 2026-10-12)

#### `world.1900.technology.phonograph-gramophone` · description · corrected

> Phonograph and gramophone: Wind-up machines played wax cylinders or flat discs through a large horn; records had been on sale since the 1890s.

- **Old:** Hand-cranked machines played wax cylinders or flat discs through a large horn: recorded music at home for the first time.
- **New:** Wind-up machines played wax cylinders or flat discs through a large horn; records had been on sale since the 1890s.
- **Note:** Both cylinders and discs were on sale (Berliner discs from 1894; Lambert celluloid cylinders 1900). 'Recorded music at home for the first time' was wrong for 1900: recordings had been sold since the 1890s.
- **Source:** [Wikipedia: Phonograph record](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phonograph_record) (reference, 2026-10-12)
- **Source:** [Wikipedia: Phonograph cylinder](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phonograph_cylinder) (reference, 2026-10-12)

#### `world.1900.technology.safety-bicycle` · description · corrected

> Safety bicycle: The safety bicycle, with two equal wheels and a chain drive, set off the bicycle boom of the 1890s.

- **Old:** The bicycle craze of the 1890s put people on two wheels; for many it was the fastest way to get around town.
- **New:** The safety bicycle, with two equal wheels and a chain drive, set off the bicycle boom of the 1890s.
- **Note:** The safety bicycle caused the bike boom of the 1890s. 'Fastest way to get around town' was unsourced and removed.
- **Source:** [Wikipedia: Safety bicycle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Safety_bicycle) (reference, 2026-10-12)

#### `world.1900.technology.telephone` · description · corrected

> Telephone: About 1.4 million telephones in 1900, fewer than 2 for every 100 people, so most farm families had none. Calls went through a switchboard operator.

- **Old:** Spreading in towns and cities, but most farm families did not have one yet. Calls went through a local switchboard operator.
- **New:** About 1.4 million telephones in 1900, fewer than 2 for every 100 people, so most farm families had none. Calls went through a switchboard operator.
- **Note:** Series R 1–2: 1,356,000 telephones in 1900, 17.6 per 1,000 people. The operator detail is general knowledge for the period (automatic exchanges were rare).
- **Source:** [U.S. Census Bureau, Historical Statistics of the United States, Colonial Times to 1970, series R 1–12: telephones](https://www2.census.gov/library/publications/1975/compendia/hist_stats_colonial-1970/hist_stats_colonial-1970p2-chR.pdf) (primary, 2026-10-12)

#### `world.1900.technology.typewriter` · description · corrected

> Typewriter: Sold since 1874; newer machines such as the 1896 Underwood let typists see their words as they typed.

- **Old:** The modern office machine, rapidly replacing handwritten business letters.
- **New:** Sold since 1874; newer machines such as the 1896 Underwood let typists see their words as they typed.
- **Note:** Commercial typewriters were sold from 1874; the 1896 Underwood made typing visible. 'Rapidly replacing handwritten letters' was unsourced.
- **Source:** [Wikipedia: Typewriter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Typewriter) (reference, 2026-10-12)

#### `world.1900.visions.americans-will-be-taller` · cameTrue · corrected

> cameTrue = yes (Even more than predicted: men born around 1900 averaged about 170 cm, men born in the 1950s–1980s about 177–179 cm, roughly 3 inches taller.)

- **Old:** cameTrue = yes (Average heights did rise by roughly that much over the century.)
- **New:** cameTrue = yes (Even more than predicted: men born around 1900 averaged about 170 cm, men born in the 1950s–1980s about 177–179 cm, roughly 3 inches taller.)
- **Note:** US men born in 1900 averaged 170 cm; those born in 1950–1980 averaged 177–179 cm, a gain of about 3 inches.
- **Source:** [Our World in Data: Average height of men by year of birth, United States (NCD-RisC and Baten & Blum data)](https://ourworldindata.org/grapher/average-height-of-men-for-selected-countries) (reference, 2026-10-12)

#### `world.1900.visions.photographs-will-be-telegraphed` · cameTrue · corrected

> cameTrue = yes (Western Union wired its first photograph in 1921 and a news wirephoto network began in 1935; today photos travel instantly.)

- **Old:** cameTrue = yes (Wire photos came within decades; today photos travel instantly.)
- **New:** cameTrue = yes (Western Union wired its first photograph in 1921 and a news wirephoto network began in 1935; today photos travel instantly.)
- **Note:** Western Union sent its first photograph by wire in 1921 and AP's Wirephoto network started in 1935.
- **Source:** [Wikipedia: Wirephoto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wirephoto) (reference, 2026-10-12)

#### `world.1900.morning.walk-one-room-schoolhouse` · detail · verified

> 7:30 AM Walk to the one-room schoolhouse: Children of all ages often shared one room and one teacher, walking there along country roads.

- **Note:** Even in 1929–30, 149,282 of 238,306 public elementary schools (63%) were one-teacher schools; with a larger rural share in 1900, one-room schools were the norm for farm children. The source starts in 1929–30, so this is a bound, not a 1900 count.
- **Source:** [NCES, 120 Years of American Education: A Statistical Portrait (1993), Table 20](https://nces.ed.gov/pubs93/93442.pdf) (primary, 2026-10-12)

#### `world.1900.places.bedroom` · summary · verified

### world · 1920

#### `world.1920.morning.catch-streetcar` · detail · corrected

> 7:40 AM Catch the streetcar: Electric streetcars ran down the middle of city streets. Cars were spreading fast, but there was only about one motor vehicle for every 11 Americans.

- **Old:** Electric streetcars carried most city commuters. Cars were spreading fast, but most families did not yet own one.
- **New:** Electric streetcars ran down the middle of city streets. Cars were spreading fast, but there was only about one motor vehicle for every 11 Americans.
- **Note:** 9,239,161 motor vehicles (FHWA) for 106,021,537 people (Census) is about one for every 11 people. 'Streetcars carried most city commuters' had no source and was removed.
- **Source:** [FHWA Highway Statistics Summary to 1995, Table MV-200: State Motor Vehicle Registrations, 1900–1995](https://www.fhwa.dot.gov/ohim/summary95/mv200.pdf) (primary, 2026-10-12)
- **Source:** [U.S. Census Bureau decennial counts, Urban and Rural Population for the U.S. and all States: 1900–2000 (State Data Center of Iowa)](https://www.iowadatacenter.org/datatables/UnitedStates/urusstpop19002000.pdf) (primary, 2026-10-12)

#### `world.1920.morning.shave-wash-up` · detail · corrected

> 6:30 AM Shave and wash up: A safety razor with throwaway double-edged blades, the kind issued to American soldiers in the war, and a basin of hot water from the kettle.

- **Old:** A safety razor and a basin of hot water from the kettle. Indoor bathrooms were common in cities but far from universal in the countryside.
- **New:** A safety razor with throwaway double-edged blades, the kind issued to American soldiers in the war, and a basin of hot water from the kettle.
- **Note:** Gillette's disposable double-edge blade razor was patented in 1904 and supplied to U.S. troops in the First World War. The unsourced claim about indoor bathrooms was removed.
- **Source:** [Wikipedia: Safety razor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Safety_razor) (reference, 2026-10-12)

#### `world.1920.places.newspaper` · description · corrected

> Broadsheets packed narrow columns with stacked headlines. In 1920 the big stories included Prohibition, women winning the vote, and KDKA's radio broadcast of the election returns, often cited as the start of commercial radio.

- **Old:** Broadsheets packed narrow columns with small type, stacked headlines and few photographs. In 1920 the big stories included Prohibition, women winning the vote, and KDKA's radio broadcast of the election returns, often called the start of commercial radio.
- **New:** Broadsheets packed narrow columns with stacked headlines. In 1920 the big stories included Prohibition, women winning the vote, and KDKA's radio broadcast of the election returns, often cited as the start of commercial radio.
- **Note:** Dates checked: Prohibition took effect 17 January; the 19th Amendment was ratified 18 August and certified 26 August (National Archives); KDKA's broadcast and Harding's election were 2 November. 'Few photographs' was unsourced and dropped; KDKA's 'first' claim is disputed (WWJ Detroit), so the wording now says it is often cited.
- **Source:** [Wikipedia: 1920 in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1920_in_the_United_States) (reference, 2026-10-12)
- **Source:** [National Archives: 19th Amendment to the U.S. Constitution: Women's Right to Vote (1920)](https://www.archives.gov/milestone-documents/19th-amendment) (primary, 2026-10-12)
- **Source:** [Wikipedia: KDKA (AM)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/KDKA_(AM)) (reference, 2026-10-12)

#### `world.1920.scene.caption` · caption · corrected

> An early wooden radio receiver. Broadcasting had only just begun, and most listeners were hobbyists wearing headphones.

- **Old:** A wooden tabletop radio, the new voice in the parlour.
- **New:** An early wooden radio receiver. Broadcasting had only just begun, and most listeners were hobbyists wearing headphones.
- **Note:** A wooden table radio was not yet a typical home item in 1920: broadcasting only began that November and early listeners used crystal sets and headphones.
- **Source:** [Wikipedia: KDKA (AM)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/KDKA_(AM)) (reference, 2026-10-12)
- **Source:** [Wikipedia: Crystal radio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crystal_radio) (reference, 2026-10-12)

#### `world.1920.technology.candlestick-telephone` · description · corrected

> Candlestick telephone: An upright mouthpiece with a separate earpiece on a hook. About 35 in 100 homes had a telephone, and most calls went through a switchboard operator.

- **Old:** An upright mouthpiece with a separate earpiece on a hook. Most calls went through a human switchboard operator.
- **New:** An upright mouthpiece with a separate earpiece on a hook. About 35 in 100 homes had a telephone, and most calls went through a switchboard operator.
- **Note:** Candlestick phones were common from the late 1890s to the 1940s. Added the share of homes with a phone: 35.0% in 1920 (series R 3).
- **Source:** [Wikipedia: Candlestick telephone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Candlestick_telephone) (reference, 2026-10-12)
- **Source:** [U.S. Census Bureau, Historical Statistics of the United States, Colonial Times to 1970, series R 1–12: telephones](https://www2.census.gov/library/publications/1975/compendia/hist_stats_colonial-1970/hist_stats_colonial-1970p2-chR.pdf) (primary, 2026-10-12)
- **Source:** [Wikipedia: Rotary dial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rotary_dial) (reference, 2026-10-12)

#### `world.1920.technology.electric-iron-vacuum-cleaner` · description · corrected

> Electric iron and vacuum cleaner: About a third of homes had electricity in 1920. In those that did, electric irons and vacuum cleaners began replacing flat irons heated on the stove and carpet beaters.

- **Old:** For homes that were wired, electric appliances began replacing flat irons heated on the stove and carpet beaters.
- **New:** About a third of homes had electricity in 1920. In those that did, electric irons and vacuum cleaners began replacing flat irons heated on the stove and carpet beaters.
- **Note:** Electrification share verified (series S 109). The appliances existed (vacuum cleaners from the 1900s); how widely they replaced older tools is not sourced.
- **Source:** [U.S. Census Bureau, Historical Statistics of the United States, Colonial Times to 1970, series S 108–119: dwellings with electric service](https://www2.census.gov/library/publications/1975/compendia/hist_stats_colonial-1970/hist_stats_colonial-1970p2-chS.pdf) (primary, 2026-10-12)
- **Source:** [Wikipedia: Vacuum cleaner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vacuum_cleaner) (reference, 2026-10-12)

### world · 1930

#### `world.1930.morning.scan-want-ads` · detail · corrected

> 7:00 AM Scan the want ads: Unemployment had jumped from 3.2% in 1929 to 8.7% in 1930, more than 4 million people, so the job pages were read closely.

- **Old:** With unemployment rising after the 1929 crash, the job pages were read closely.
- **New:** Unemployment had jumped from 3.2% in 1929 to 8.7% in 1930, more than 4 million people, so the job pages were read closely.
- **Note:** Series D 86: unemployment 3.2% in 1929, 8.7% in 1930 (4.3 million people), heading to 24.9% in 1933.
- **Source:** [U.S. Census Bureau, Historical Statistics of the United States, Colonial Times to 1970, series D 85–86: unemployment](https://www2.census.gov/library/publications/1975/compendia/hist_stats_colonial-1970/hist_stats_colonial-1970p1-chD.pdf) (primary, 2026-10-12)

#### `world.1930.morning.subway-office` · detail · corrected

> 7:30 AM Subway to the office: A ride of any length on the subway or elevated trains cost a nickel, as it had since 1904.

- **Old:** Most New Yorkers rode the subway or elevated trains to work.
- **New:** A ride of any length on the subway or elevated trains cost a nickel, as it had since 1904.
- **Note:** The 5-cent fare for any length of ride ran from 1904 to 1 July 1948. 'Most New Yorkers rode the subway' had no source and was removed.
- **Source:** [Wikipedia: New York City transit fares](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_York_City_transit_fares) (reference, 2026-10-12)

#### `world.1930.morning.wake-radio` · detail · corrected

> 6:30 AM Wake to the radio: The 1930 census counted radios in about 12 million homes, 40% of all households.

- **Old:** In 1930, 40% of American households owned a radio, and far more in big cities.
- **New:** The 1930 census counted radios in about 12 million homes, 40% of all households.
- **Note:** The 1930 census counted 12,049,000 households with a radio (series R 104), about 40% of households (Wikipedia citing the 1930 census abstract). 'Far more in big cities' was unsourced and removed.
- **Source:** [U.S. Census Bureau, Historical Statistics of the United States, Colonial Times to 1970, series R 93–105: households with radio and television sets](https://www2.census.gov/library/publications/1975/compendia/hist_stats_colonial-1970/hist_stats_colonial-1970p2-chR.pdf) (primary, 2026-10-12)
- **Source:** [Wikipedia: Golden Age of Radio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Golden_Age_of_Radio) (reference, 2026-10-12)

#### `world.1930.places.newspaper` · description · corrected

> The news mixed wonder and worry: a new planet, new towers and a worsening economy.

- **Old:** Bold headline type and more photographs. The news mixed wonder and worry: a new planet, new towers and a worsening economy.
- **New:** The news mixed wonder and worry: a new planet, new towers and a worsening economy.
- **Note:** Dates checked: Pluto found 18 February and announced 13 March; Chrysler Building completed 27 May; Smoot–Hawley signed 17 June; Hoover asked Congress for a $150 million public works program on 2 December. 'Bold headline type and more photographs' was unsourced and dropped.
- **Source:** [Wikipedia: 1930 in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1930_in_the_United_States) (reference, 2026-10-12)
- **Source:** [Wikipedia: Pluto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pluto) (reference, 2026-10-12)
- **Source:** [Wikipedia: Smoot–Hawley Tariff Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Smoot%E2%80%93Hawley_Tariff_Act) (reference, 2026-10-12)

#### `world.1930.prices.ford-model-tudor-sedan` · price · corrected

> Ford Model A Tudor sedan (1929 list price): $500

- **Old:** {"price":"about $500","item":"Ford Model A Tudor sedan"}
- **New:** {"price":"$500","item":"Ford Model A Tudor sedan (1929 list price)"}
- **Note:** The source gives $500 for the Tudor in 1929, the nearest year with a sourced price; the label now says so.
- **Source:** [Wikipedia: Ford Model A (1927–1932)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ford_Model_A_(1927%E2%80%931932)) (reference, 2026-10-12)

#### `world.1930.prices.loaf-bread-1-lb` · price · corrected

> Bread (1 lb, city average): 8.6¢

- **Old:** {"price":"about $0.09","item":"Loaf of bread (1 lb, New York City)"}
- **New:** {"price":"8.6¢","item":"Bread (1 lb, city average)"}
- **Note:** National city average replaces the New York figure: series E 188, 1930 = 8.6¢ per pound.
- **Source:** [U.S. Census Bureau, Historical Statistics of the United States, Colonial Times to 1970, series E 187–202: retail food prices in U.S. cities (BLS)](https://www2.census.gov/library/publications/1975/compendia/hist_stats_colonial-1970/hist_stats_colonial-1970p1-chE.pdf) (primary, 2026-10-12)

#### `world.1930.technology.electric-refrigerator` · description · corrected

> Electric refrigerator: Sold since 1915 and catching on during the 1930s, the electric refrigerator was starting to replace the icebox.

- **Old:** Starting to replace the icebox in better-off homes; still a luxury for most.
- **New:** Sold since 1915 and catching on during the 1930s, the electric refrigerator was starting to replace the icebox.
- **Note:** Practical home refrigerators were introduced in 1915 and gained wider acceptance in the U.S. in the 1930s. 'Better-off homes' and 'luxury' were unsourced.
- **Source:** [Wikipedia: Refrigerator](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Refrigerator) (reference, 2026-10-12)

#### `world.1930.technology.ford-model` · description · corrected

> Ford Model A: Ford's successor to the Model T; two million had been sold by July 1929.

- **Old:** Ford's successor to the Model T; by March 1930 sales had passed three million.
- **New:** Ford's successor to the Model T; two million had been sold by July 1929.
- **Note:** The source supports one million sold by 4 February 1929 and two million by 24 July 1929; 'three million by March 1930' was not found.
- **Source:** [Wikipedia: Ford Model A (1927–1932)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ford_Model_A_(1927%E2%80%931932)) (reference, 2026-10-12)

#### `world.1930.technology.talking-pictures` · description · corrected

> Talking pictures: Studios had stopped making purely silent features, though many films still came in silent versions for theatres not yet wired for sound.

- **Old:** Films with synchronized sound had all but replaced silent pictures in new releases.
- **New:** Studios had stopped making purely silent features, though many films still came in silent versions for theatres not yet wired for sound.
- **Note:** The last purely silent major-studio feature came out in August 1929, but until mid-1930 most Hollywood films were also made in silent versions for theatres not yet wired for sound.
- **Source:** [Wikipedia: Sound film](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sound_film) (reference, 2026-10-12)

#### `world.1930.visions.century-rising-productivity-will` · cameTrue · corrected

> cameTrue = no (Not on track: full-time work weeks are still far longer than fifteen hours, four years before his deadline.)

- **Old:** cameTrue = no (Productivity rose roughly as he expected, but working hours fell far less.)
- **New:** cameTrue = no (Not on track: full-time work weeks are still far longer than fifteen hours, four years before his deadline.)
- **Note:** Verdict kept as 'no'; note reworded because his 100-year horizon is 2030. Manufacturing weekly hours were still 39.8 in 1970 (series D 803).
- **Source:** [U.S. Census Bureau, Historical Statistics of the United States, Colonial Times to 1970, series D 802–810: earnings and hours of production workers in manufacturing](https://www2.census.gov/library/publications/1975/compendia/hist_stats_colonial-1970/hist_stats_colonial-1970p1-chD.pdf) (primary, 2026-10-12)

#### `world.1930.visions.century-rising-productivity-will` · prediction · corrected

> A hundred years from now, rising productivity may let people work three-hour shifts, a fifteen-hour week. (John Maynard Keynes, "Economic Possibilities for our Grandchildren", 1930)

- **Old:** {"prediction":"In a century, rising productivity will let people work about fifteen hours a week.","whoSaidIt":"John Maynard Keynes, \"Economic Possibilities for our Grandchildren\" (1930)"}
- **New:** {"prediction":"A hundred years from now, rising productivity may let people work three-hour shifts, a fifteen-hour week.","whoSaidIt":"John Maynard Keynes, \"Economic Possibilities for our Grandchildren\", 1930"}
- **Note:** Keynes wrote 'Three-hour shifts or a fifteen-hour week may put off the problem for a great while' about the world a hundred years after 1930. Wording adjusted to match.
- **Source:** [John Maynard Keynes, "Economic Possibilities for our Grandchildren" (1930), full text (Yale course reader)](http://www.econ.yale.edu/smith/econ116a/keynes1.pdf) (primary, 2026-10-12)

### world · 1945

#### `world.1945.morning.check-victory-garden` · detail · corrected

> 7:00 AM Check the victory garden: Families grew vegetables in backyards and vacant lots; victory gardens grew about a third of the country's vegetables during the war.

- **Old:** Families grew vegetables in backyards and vacant lots to stretch rations.
- **New:** Families grew vegetables in backyards and vacant lots; victory gardens grew about a third of the country's vegetables during the war.
- **Note:** The USDA encouraged victory gardens, and about a third of U.S. vegetables came from them during the war.
- **Source:** [Wikipedia: Victory garden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Victory_garden) (reference, 2026-10-12)

#### `world.1945.morning.up-early-shift` · detail · corrected

> 5:30 AM Up for the early shift: Millions of women worked in war industries: more than six million took war jobs.

- **Old:** War plants ran around the clock, and many women worked factory shifts.
- **New:** Millions of women worked in war industries: more than six million took war jobs.
- **Note:** Nearly 19 million women held jobs during the war, and over six million took war jobs. 'Plants ran around the clock' had no source and was removed.
- **Source:** [Wikipedia: Rosie the Riveter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rosie_the_Riveter) (reference, 2026-10-12)

#### `world.1945.technology.eniac` · description · corrected

> ENIAC: The electronic computer was completed in 1945 and put to its first practical work on December 10.

- **Old:** Assembly of the electronic computer was completed on November 29, 1945, and it ran its first calculations.
- **New:** The electronic computer was completed in 1945 and put to its first practical work on December 10.
- **Note:** ENIAC was completed in 1945 and first put to practical work on 10 December 1945; the specific 29 November date was not in the source.
- **Source:** [Wikipedia: ENIAC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ENIAC) (reference, 2026-10-12)

- **Old:** In 83% of homes by 1940; families gathered around it for war news and the president's addresses.
- **New:** In 83% of homes by 1940 and 33 million households in 1945; families gathered around it for war news and the president's addresses.

- **Source:** [Wikipedia: Golden Age of Radio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Golden_Age_of_Radio) (reference, 2026-10-12)

#### `world.1945.technology.penicillin` · description · corrected

> Penicillin: Mass-produced for the armed forces, it became widely available to civilians after the war ended in 1945.

- **Old:** Mass-produced for the war, it was made available to the general American public on 15 March 1945.
- **New:** Mass-produced for the armed forces, it became widely available to civilians after the war ended in 1945.
- **Note:** The date '15 March 1945' for public availability was not found in the sources opened; the history says penicillin became widely available after the war ended in 1945.
- **Source:** [Wikipedia: History of penicillin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_penicillin) (reference, 2026-10-12)

### world · 1960

#### `world.1960.era.tagline` · tagline · corrected

> Suburbs, tail fins and a television in most living rooms.

- **Old:** Suburbs, tail fins and a television in every living room.
- **New:** Suburbs, tail fins and a television in most living rooms.
- **Note:** Series R 105: 45.75 million households had a TV in 1960, not every living room. Tail fins and suburbs are context.
- **Source:** [U.S. Census Bureau, Historical Statistics of the United States, Colonial Times to 1970, series R 93–105: households with radio and television sets](https://www2.census.gov/library/publications/1975/compendia/hist_stats_colonial-1970/hist_stats_colonial-1970p2-chR.pdf) (primary, 2026-10-12)
- **Source:** [FHWA Highway Statistics Summary to 1995, Table MV-200: State Motor Vehicle Registrations, 1900–1995](https://www.fhwa.dot.gov/ohim/summary95/mv200.pdf) (primary, 2026-10-12)

#### `world.1960.era.title` · title · corrected

> The Television Sixties

- **Old:** The Space-Age Sixties
- **New:** The Television Sixties
- **Note:** 1960 was before the first American in space (1961), so 'Space-Age' overstated the year; NASA did announce Apollo in July 1960. Renamed to what the sources support.
- **Source:** [Wikipedia: Apollo program](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apollo_program) (reference, 2026-10-12)

#### `world.1960.morning.drive-suburbs` · detail · corrected

> 7:45 AM Drive in from the suburbs: Gasoline averaged about 31 cents a gallon and 74 million motor vehicles were on the road, so suburban families drove to work.

- **Old:** New highways and cheap gasoline made the car commute the norm for suburban families.
- **New:** Gasoline averaged about 31 cents a gallon and 74 million motor vehicles were on the road, so suburban families drove to work.
- **Note:** Regular gasoline averaged 31.1¢ a gallon in 1960 (EIA); 73.9 million motor vehicles were registered (FHWA). 'The car commute was the norm' was not sourced.
- **Source:** [U.S. Energy Information Administration, Annual Energy Review, Table 5.24: Retail Motor Gasoline and On-Highway Diesel Fuel Prices, 1949–2011](https://www.eia.gov/totalenergy/data/annual/showtext.php?t=ptb0524) (primary, 2026-10-12)
- **Source:** [FHWA Highway Statistics Summary to 1995, Table MV-200: State Motor Vehicle Registrations, 1900–1995](https://www.fhwa.dot.gov/ohim/summary95/mv200.pdf) (primary, 2026-10-12)

#### `world.1960.morning.morning-tv-news` · detail · corrected

> 7:00 AM Morning TV news: Breakfast-time television in black and white: morning shows had been on the air since 1952, and about 46 million households owned a set.

- **Old:** Breakfast-time television in black and white, on a set the whole family shares.
- **New:** Breakfast-time television in black and white: morning shows had been on the air since 1952, and about 46 million households owned a set.
- **Note:** NBC's Today had been on the air since 14 January 1952; 45.75 million households had a TV in 1960.
- **Source:** [Wikipedia: Today (American TV program)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Today_(American_TV_program)) (reference, 2026-10-12)
- **Source:** [U.S. Census Bureau, Historical Statistics of the United States, Colonial Times to 1970, series R 93–105: households with radio and television sets](https://www2.census.gov/library/publications/1975/compendia/hist_stats_colonial-1970/hist_stats_colonial-1970p2-chR.pdf) (primary, 2026-10-12)

- **Old:** Bigger photos and bolder type. The Cold War, civil rights and the Kennedy–Nixon election, with the first series of televised presidential debates, dominated the news.
- **New:** The Cold War, civil rights and the Kennedy–Nixon election, with the first televised presidential debates, dominated the news.
- **Note:** Dates checked: Greensboro sit-ins began 1 February; U-2 shot down 1 May; first televised debate 26 September; Kennedy elected 8 November. 'Bigger photos and bolder type' was unsourced and dropped.

- **New:** Room-sized machines fed with punched cards; new business models announced in late 1959 brought them to banks, insurers and government offices.

#### `world.1960.technology.rotary-dial-telephone` · description · corrected

> Rotary-dial telephone: A heavy desk phone in about 78% of homes. Long distance was expensive: three daytime minutes from New York to San Francisco cost $2.25.

- **Old:** A heavy desk phone, usually rented from the phone company. Long-distance calls were expensive.
- **New:** A heavy desk phone in about 78% of homes. Long distance was expensive: three daytime minutes from New York to San Francisco cost $2.25.

- **Source:** [U.S. Census Bureau, Historical Statistics of the United States, Colonial Times to 1970, series R 13–16: telephone toll rates from New York City](https://www2.census.gov/library/publications/1975/compendia/hist_stats_colonial-1970/hist_stats_colonial-1970p2-chR.pdf) (primary, 2026-10-12)

> 8:00 AM Kids catch the school bus: Suburban children rode buses to large consolidated schools; fewer than 2,000 one-teacher schools were left in the country.

- **New:** Suburban children rode buses to large consolidated schools; fewer than 2,000 one-teacher schools were left in the country.

#### `world.1984.era.tagline` · tagline · corrected

- **Old:** Beige computers, cassette tapes and the arcade down the street.
- **New:** Beige computers, cassette tapes and a phone the size of a brick.
- **Note:** Computers and cassettes verified; 'the arcade down the street' had no source and was replaced with the first handheld mobile phone (widely available April 1984).

- **Old:** Sony's Walkman, introduced in the U.S. in June 1980, made cassette music personal and portable.
- **New:** The Walkman personal cassette player, on sale since 1979, made music personal and portable.
- **Note:** The Walkman went on sale on 1 July 1979 and was first sold in the U.S. under another name; the 'June 1980' U.S. date was not in the source.

### world · 1995

#### `world.1995.era.tagline` · tagline · corrected

> Dial-up tones, home pages and new online shops.

- **Old:** Dial-up tones, home pages and the first online shops.
- **New:** Dial-up tones, home pages and new online shops.
- **Note:** Amazon and eBay launched in 1995, but they were not literally the first online shops; reworded.
- **Source:** [Wikipedia: Amazon (company)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amazon_(company)) (reference, 2026-10-12)
- **Source:** [Wikipedia: eBay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/EBay) (reference, 2026-10-12)

#### `world.1995.places.newspaper` · description · corrected

> Newspapers were still the main source of news, selling about 58 million copies each weekday. 1995 brought tragedy in Oklahoma City, a record stock market and a new version of Windows.

- **Old:** Newspapers were still the main source of news, but many started their first websites. 1995 brought tragedy in Oklahoma City, a record stock market and a new version of Windows.
- **New:** Newspapers were still the main source of news, selling about 58 million copies each weekday. 1995 brought tragedy in Oklahoma City, a record stock market and a new version of Windows.
- **Note:** Dates checked: Oklahoma City bombing 19 April (168 killed, including 19 children); Windows 95 retail 24 August; Dow first closed above 5,000 on 21 November (5,023.55); Toy Story released 22 November. 'Many started their first websites' was unsourced; replaced with Pew's circulation figure (58.2 million weekday copies in 1995).
- **Source:** [Wikipedia: 1995 in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1995_in_the_United_States) (reference, 2026-10-12)
- **Source:** [Wikipedia: Windows 95](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Windows_95) (reference, 2026-10-12)
- **Source:** [Pew Research Center: Newspapers Fact Sheet (total U.S. daily newspaper circulation)](https://www.pewresearch.org/journalism/fact-sheet/newspapers/) (primary, 2026-10-12)

#### `world.1995.technology.dial-up-modem` · description · corrected

> Dial-up modem: Modems on the phone line connected at up to 28.8 kbit/s under a standard approved in 1994; many older ones ran at 14.4 kbit/s.

- **Old:** 28.8k modems connected over the phone line with a famous screech.
- **New:** Modems on the phone line connected at up to 28.8 kbit/s under a standard approved in 1994; many older ones ran at 14.4 kbit/s.
- **Note:** V.34 (September 1994) allowed up to 28.8 kbit/s; V.32bis (1991) 14.4 kbit/s. The 'famous screech' is description.
- **Source:** [Wikipedia: List of ITU-T V-series recommendations (V.34)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_ITU-T_V-series_recommendations) (reference, 2026-10-12)

#### `world.1995.visions.predictions-that-well-soon` · cameTrue · corrected

> cameTrue = no (U.S. daily newspaper circulation fell from about 58 million on weekdays in 1995 to 21 million in 2022, print and digital combined.)

- **Old:** cameTrue = no (Amazon began selling books online that July.)
- **New:** cameTrue = no (U.S. daily newspaper circulation fell from about 58 million on weekdays in 1995 to 21 million in 2022, print and digital combined.)
- **Note:** Pew: weekday circulation 58,193,000 (1995); 20.9 million (2022).
- **Source:** [Pew Research Center: Newspapers Fact Sheet (total U.S. daily newspaper circulation)](https://www.pewresearch.org/journalism/fact-sheet/newspapers/) (primary, 2026-10-12)

#### `world.1995.visions.predictions-that-well-soon` · prediction · corrected

> "No online database will replace your daily newspaper", and online catalogue shopping won't make stores obsolete. (Clifford Stoll, "The Internet? Bah!", Newsweek, February 1995)

- **Old:** {"prediction":"Predictions that we'll soon buy books and newspapers straight over the Internet are hype.","whoSaidIt":"Clifford Stoll, \"The Internet? Bah!\", Newsweek, February 27, 1995"}
- **New:** {"prediction":"\"No online database will replace your daily newspaper\", and online catalogue shopping won't make stores obsolete.","whoSaidIt":"Clifford Stoll, \"The Internet? Bah!\", Newsweek, February 1995"}
- **Note:** The old wording paraphrased Stoll as calling online books and newspapers 'hype'. His column says: 'no online database will replace your daily newspaper', and doubts that online shopping will make stores obsolete. Now quotes him. Newsweek's site dates the column 26 February 1995.
- **Source:** [Clifford Stoll, "Why the Web Won't Be Nirvana" ("The Internet? Bah!"), Newsweek, posted 26 February 1995](https://www.newsweek.com/clifford-stoll-why-web-wont-be-nirvana-185306) (primary, 2026-10-12)

#### `world.1999.technology.56k-dial-up-modem` · description · corrected

> 56k dial-up modem: Connected through the phone line at up to 56 kbit/s downloading; a 4 MB music file took about 10 minutes even at full speed.

- **Old:** Connected through the phone line; a single song could take many minutes to download.
- **New:** Connected through the phone line at up to 56 kbit/s downloading; a 4 MB music file took about 10 minutes even at full speed.
- **Note:** V.90: 56 kbit/s download, 33.6 kbit/s upload. 'A song could take many minutes' replaced with the arithmetic: a 4 MB file takes about 10 minutes at 56 kbit/s even at full speed.

- **New:** cameTrue = no (Broadcasters dropped 3D channels by 2013 and most manufacturers had stopped making 3D sets by 2017.)

### world · 2020

#### `world.2020.morning.commute-bedroom-kitchen-table` · detail · corrected

> 7:00 AM Commute: bedroom to kitchen table: After the national emergency was declared on March 13 and states issued stay-at-home orders, many people worked from home.

- **Old:** After the U.S. declared a national emergency on March 13, many offices and schools moved online.
- **New:** After the national emergency was declared on March 13 and states issued stay-at-home orders, many people worked from home.
- **Note:** Proclamation 9994 was signed 13 March 2020; states imposed stay-at-home orders in March and early April. Wording tied to those facts.
- **Source:** [Proclamation 9994 of March 13, 2020: Declaring a National Emergency Concerning the Novel Coronavirus Disease (COVID-19) Outbreak, Federal Register 85 FR 15337](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/FR-2020-03-18/pdf/2020-05794.pdf) (primary, 2026-10-12)
- **Source:** [Wikipedia: COVID-19 pandemic in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic_in_the_United_States) (reference, 2026-10-12)

#### `world.2020.morning.walk-mask-pocket` · detail · corrected

> 12:00 PM A walk, mask in pocket: From April 3 the CDC recommended cloth face coverings in public places such as grocery stores, so masks became part of leaving the house.

- **Old:** Masks became part of leaving the house.
- **New:** From April 3 the CDC recommended cloth face coverings in public places such as grocery stores, so masks became part of leaving the house.
- **Note:** The CDC recommended non-medical face coverings in public on 3 April 2020.
- **Source:** [Wikipedia: Face masks during the COVID-19 pandemic in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Face_masks_during_the_COVID-19_pandemic_in_the_United_States) (reference, 2026-10-12)
